=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given two length-N strings A = A_1A_2 \ldots A_N and B = B_1B_2 \ldots B_N, each consisting of 0 and 1.
There are N squares aligned in a row from left to right. For i = 1, 2, \ldots, N, the i-th square from the left is called square i. Initially, square i contains a piece if A_i =  1, and no piece if A_i =  0.
You may repeat the following operation any number of times (possibly zero):

- Choose an integer i between 1 and N, inclusive.
- Move all pieces simultaneously one square closer to square i. That is, for each piece, let square j be its current position and square j' be its new position, and the following holds:
- if i < j, then j' = j-1;
- if i > j, then j' = j+1;
- if i = j, then j' = j.



Determine whether it is possible to reach a configuration satisfying the following condition, and if it is possible, find the minimum number of operations needed to do so:

For every i = 1, 2, \ldots, N, there is at least one piece in square i if and only if B_i =  1.

You are given T independent test cases. Print the answer for each of them.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
T
\mathrm{case}_1
\mathrm{case}_2
\vdots
\mathrm{case}_T

Here, \mathrm{case}_i (i=1,2,\ldots,T) denotes the i-th test case.
Each test case is given in the following format:
N
A
B

Output

Print T lines.
For each i = 1, 2, \ldots, T, on the i-th line, print -1 if it is impossible to reach a configuration satisfying the condition for the i-th test case. Otherwise, print the minimum number of operations needed.

Constraints


- 1 \leq T \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq N \leq 10^6
- T and N are integers.
- A and B are strings of length N, each consisting of 0 and 1.
- There exists i such that A_i =  1.
- There exists i such that B_i =  1.
- The sum of N over all test cases is at most 10^6.

Sample Input 1

3
8
01001101
00001011
3
010
111
20
10100011011110101011
00010001111101100000

Sample Output 1

3
-1
5

The input has three independent test cases.
In the first test case, initially, the sequence of the numbers of pieces in the squares is (0, 1, 0, 0, 1, 1, 0, 1). By performing the operation three times as follows, you can satisfy the condition:

- Choose i = 5. After the operation, the configuration is (0, 0, 1, 0, 2, 0, 1, 0).
- Choose i = 8. After the operation, the configuration is (0, 0, 0, 1, 0, 2, 0, 1).
- Choose i = 8. After the operation, the configuration is (0, 0, 0, 0, 1, 0, 2, 1).

It is impossible to satisfy the condition in fewer than three operations, so the answer is 3.
In the second test case, no matter how you perform the operations, you cannot satisfy the condition, so the answer is -1.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

For two pieces, operation target i:
- If i < p1: both move left.
- If i > p2: both move right.
- If p1 < i < p2: p1 right, p2 left (toward each other), gap decreases by 2.
- If i=p1: p1 stays, p2 moves left (if p2>p1), gap decreases by1.
- If i=p2: p1 moves right, p2 stays, gap decreases by1.
- If i between? integer.

So to move both same direction, choose target outside; gap unchanged. To shrink gap, choose target inside (or at endpoint). Gap cannot increase. Thus if final support has two distinct squares, gap final <= initial gap. If final one square, gap 0.

For two pieces, state (x,y), x<=y. Operation:
- outside left: (x-1,y-1)
- outside right: (x+1,y+1)
- inside target i with x<i<y: (x+1,y-1)
- target x: (x,y-1)
- target y: (x+1,y)
So each operation can change x by -1,0,+1 and y by -1,0,+1, with constraints:
- both move same direction if target outside: Δx=Δy=±1.
- if target between: Δx=+1, Δy=-1 (gap -2).
- target x: Δx=0, Δy=-1.
- target y: Δx=+1, Δy=0.
Thus gap d=y-x changes by:
- outside: 0
- between: -2
- endpoint: -1
So gap can decrease by 0,1,2 per operation, never increase. To achieve final gap d_f <= d_i. Need also shift.

We have K pieces sorted. Gaps g_r = p_{r+1}-p_r >=0 (initial >0). Operation effect on gaps:
- If target outside all pieces: all p_r shift same direction, gaps unchanged.
- If target inside between some pieces: pieces left of target move right, right move left. Gaps between consecutive pieces:
  * both on left: both +1, gap unchanged.
  * both on right: both -1, gap unchanged.
  * one left one right: left +1, right -1, gap decreases by 2.
  * if target equals a piece position, that piece stays; adjacent gaps may decrease by 1? Let's compute.
Thus each gap can decrease by 0,1,2 per operation? For target at a piece p_s, pieces left of target (r<s) move right, pieces right (r>s) move left, piece s stays. Gap g_{s-1}=p_s-p_{s-1}: p_s stays, p_{s-1}->+1, so gap decreases by1. Gap g_s=p_{s+1}-p_s: p_{s+1}->-1, gap decreases by1. Other gaps unchanged. If target between p_s and p_{s+1} (i = p_s + d? This gap perspective: To transform p to q, need reduce each gap g_r to h_r = q_{r+1}-q_r (<=g_r). Each operation can reduce gaps in a localized way: choosing target at a piece reduces the two adjacent gaps by 1 each (if pieces at distinct positions? Actually if target at p_s, gap left and right decrease by1). Choosing target between p_s and p_{s+1} reduces gap g_s by2. Outside no gap change but shifts all positions.

Also operations that reduce gaps also affect positions (translation). We need final positions q, not just gaps. The absolute position of first piece p_1 changes by operations. Let's track p_1. Operation:
- target < p_1 (outside left): p_1 moves left (decrease by1)
- target > p_K (outside right): p_1 moves right (increase by1)
- target inside: p_1 moves right if target > p_1 (since p_1 left of target) unless target=p_1 then stays. If target inside, p_1 (leftmost) moves right by1 (or stays if target=p_1). So p_1 never moves left except when target is left of all pieces. Similarly p_K never moves right except when target right of all pieces; inside moves left.

Thus to shift entire configuration left, choose target left of all pieces; to shift right, choose target right of all pieces. These operations don't change gaps. To shrink gaps, choose target inside, which also tends to move p_1 right and p_K left (unless target at endpoints), so it shifts endpoints inward, not whole config.

Given final q, need p_1 final = q_1. The total change in p_1 equals (# outside-right ops) - (# outside-left ops) + (# inside ops with target > p_1) (target not at p_1) maybe. But maybe can express via "area" of gaps? There is a known representation: positions p_r = p_1 + sum_{s<r} g_s. Final q similarly. Operations that change gaps and p_1.

Let's derive from sorted pieces and gaps.

Since pieces are ordered, final q support B. We need choose q_r ∈ S (B=1) with at least one per S. Let S positions s_1 < ... < s_M (M=ones(B)). Since K>=M. Final multiset q consists of M groups at positions s_m, each group size c_m >=1, sum K. Gaps h_r = q_{r+1}-q_r. Many zeros within groups, positive between groups equal s_{m+1}-s_m. Specifically if group sizes c_m, then h sequence has:
- within group m: c_m-1 zeros
- between group m and m+1: one gap = s_{m+1}-s_m
So final gap vector h is determined by group sizes: zeros inside groups, positive distances between B positions. It must satisfy h_r <= g_r for all r (componentwise) for reachability. This is key. Since gaps cannot increase. Thus a necessary and sufficient condition for reachability to some final configuration with support B may be: there exists a partition of K ordered pieces into M consecutive groups of sizes c_m>=1 such that for each boundary between groups, the final gap (distance between consecutive B squares) <= the sum of initial gaps over the pieces in the two adjacent? Wait h_r corresponds to gap between piece r and r+1. If r is the last piece of group m and r+1 first of group m+1, then h_r = s_{m+1}-s_m. For all other r (inside group), h_r=0 <= g_r automatically. So only M-1 boundary gaps need be <= corresponding initial gap g_r. That is, for each boundary between groups, the initial gap between the last piece of group m and first piece of group m+1 must be at least the distance between B positions s_m and s_{m+1}. Because final gap between those two pieces equals that distance. Since within group final gap 0.

Thus reachability to some q with support B iff we can choose M-1 cut positions among K-1 gaps such that for each cut r_m, g_{r_m} >= D_m = s_{m+1}-s_m, and groups nonempty. This is like matching B positions to blocks of pieces, where each block assigned to a B square, and adjacent B square distance must fit in the gap between blocks.

Is that sufficient? Need also absolute positions? Given gaps h <= g, can we always choose p_1 final q_1 = s_1? We can translate using outside operations without changing gaps. But if we shrink gaps to h, p_1 changes. Can we achieve exact q_1? Probably yes by interspersing outside shifts. Need prove later. But min operations may include translation.

Maybe there is a formula in terms of gaps and translation. Let's study operation count.

We have K pieces sorted. Let p_r(t). Define gaps g_r(t)=p_{r+1}-p_r. Operation effects:
- Outside right: all p_r +1; gaps unchanged; p_1 +1.
- Outside left: all p_r -1; gaps unchanged; p_1 -1.
- Target at position p_s (or within a group of duplicates): p_s stays; left pieces +1, right pieces -1. Gaps: g_{s-1} decreases by1 (if s>1), g_s decreases by1 (if s<K); other gaps unchanged. p_1 increases by1 if s>1? If target at p_s with s>1, p_1 is left of target, moves right +1. If s=1, p_1 stays. p_K decreases by1 if s<K.
- Target between p_s and p_{s+1}: p_s +1, p_{s+1} -1; gap g_s decreases by2; p_1 +1 if s>1? If s=1, p_1 moves right; p_K decreases if s<K? Actually if s between, pieces left of target (r<=s) move right, right (r>s) move left. So p_1 +1 always (since target>p_1), p_K -1 always (target<p_K). Gap g_s decreases by2. Other gaps unchanged.

Thus operations that change gaps also change p_1 (usually +1) and p_K (-1) except target at p_1 or p_K. Outside shifts change p_1 and p_K same direction.

- Target at p_1: L stays, R-- (if K>1), span decreases by1? Actually if target at leftmost, p_1 stays, all others move left, so span decreases by1 (R--). p_1 unchanged.
- Target at p_K: L++, R stays, span decreases by1.
- Target between p_s,p_{s+1}: L++, R--, span decreases by2.

Given final q, L_f and R_f fixed. Let ΔL = L_f - L_i, ΔR = R_f - R_i, ΔS = ΔR - ΔL = H-S <=0. Let counts:
a = outside right
b = outside left
c = target p1 (L0,R-1)
d = target pK (L+1,R0)
e = internal (L+1,R-1)
Then:
ΔL = a - b + d + e
ΔR = a - b - c - e
ΔS = ΔR - ΔL = -c - d - 2e = H-S (must be <=0).
Total ops t = a+b+c+d+e.

For given t, can we satisfy? This is like integer feasibility. Since outside shifts a,b contribute same to L,R and don't change span. They can adjust translation. Internal/endpoint ops reduce span and also shift L/R.

Maybe min t can be derived from ΔL, ΔR, ΔS. Let's solve ignoring gap-specific constraints.

Let U = a-b (net outside shift). Then:
ΔL = U + d + e
ΔR = U - c - e
ΔS = -c - d - 2e = H-S.

We can choose c,d,e nonnegative integers satisfying ΔS. Let s = S-H = -ΔS >=0. Need c+d+2e = s. Then U = ΔL - d - e = ΔR + c + e. Consistency requires ΔL - ΔR = c+d+2e = s, which holds. So for any c,d,e with sum weighted s, U determined. Total t = a+b+c+d+e = |U| + 2? Wait a,b nonnegative with a-b=U, minimal a+b=|U| (choose one zero). Let's simplify. Let s = S-H. We need reduce span by s. Options:

- c target p1 reduces span by1, contributes 0 to ΔL, -1 to ΔR.
- d target pK reduces span by1, contributes +1 to ΔL, 0 to ΔR.

We can think each unit of span reduction can be achieved by:
- one "left shrink" c: L same, R down 1.
- one "right shrink" d: L up 1, R same.
- one "both shrink" e: L up1, R down1, costs 2 span.

Need formulate min operations to achieve final gaps h_r <= g_r. Each operation reduces certain gaps. We need reduce each gap g_r to h_r. Since gaps never increase, final h_r = g_r - total reduction of gap r. Total reduction needed δ_r = g_r - h_r >=0. Operation types in terms of gap reductions:
- Outside: no gaps.
- Target at piece s (1-indexed): reduces gap s-1 by1 and gap s by1 (if both exist). Specifically if target at p_s, gaps adjacent to s decrease by1. If s=1, only gap1? Wait target at p1: p1 stays, p2..pK move left, so gap between p1 and p2 decreases by1. So gap1 (g_1) reduced by1. If s=K: gap K-1 reduced by1. If internal: both g_{s-1}, g_s reduced by1.
- Target between p_s and p_{s+1}: reduces gap s by2.
So each operation can reduce:
  * one gap by2 (between operation)
  * two adjacent gaps by1 each (target at a piece)
  * one boundary gap by1 (target at endpoint piece)
  * no gaps (outside shift).

This is exactly like we need reduce each gap δ_r. We can choose operations; outside shifts don't help gaps but help translation. However outside shifts also affect L,R but not gaps. Maybe min operations = min over gap-reduction schedule of (#gap-changing ops) + outside shifts needed to adjust translation. But outside shifts can be inserted and also affect L,R. Since outside shifts don't change gaps, they just translate entire configuration. They are needed to align final absolute positions after gap reductions.

Can we decouple: First perform some sequence of gap-reducing operations to achieve final gaps h (and some resulting L,R), then apply outside shifts to translate to q? Outside shifts preserve gaps, so yes any sequence can be reordered? Not necessarily, because gap-reducing operations require target positions relative to current pieces; outside shifts translate all pieces, so target positions also shift. But maybe we can think in terms of "gap-reducing operations" that reduce specified gaps; outside shifts can be applied at any time and just add to L,R. Maybe min operations = minimal number of gap-changing operations to reduce gaps to h plus minimal outside shifts to adjust translation, with possible interactions. Let's derive.

Let initial p, final q. Suppose we have a sequence of operations. Let O be number of operations that change gaps (non-outside), and U_net = a-b net outside shifts. Total t = O + a+b, with a+b >= |U_net|. Outside shifts affect L,R by U_net each. Gap-changing operations affect L,R depending on types. Let ΔL_gap, ΔR_gap from gap-changing ops. Then:
ΔL = U_net + ΔL_gap
ΔR = U_net + ΔR_gap
So U_net = ΔL - ΔL_gap = ΔR - ΔR_gap. Need consistency ΔR-ΔL = ΔR_gap-ΔL_gap = -s (span reduction), which holds. Then minimal outside ops for given gap-changing sequence is |U_net| = |ΔL - ΔL_gap|. Total = O + |ΔL - ΔL_gap|.

Thus we need choose gap-changing operations (types) that reduce each gap by δ_r, minimize O + |ΔL - ΔL_gap|, where ΔL_gap is total change in L caused by those operations. Note ΔR_gap determined by span reduction: ΔR_gap = ΔL_gap - s.

Gap-changing operation types and their contribution to ΔL_gap:
- Target at p_s:
  * s=1: reduces gap1 by1; ΔL=0.
  * s=K: reduces gap K-1 by1; ΔL=+1 (since leftmost moves right).
  * internal 1<s<K: reduces gaps s-1 and s by1; ΔL=+1.
- Target between p_s and p_{s+1}: reduces gap s by2; ΔL=+1.
So every gap-changing operation except target at p1 contributes +1 to ΔL. Target at p1 contributes 0. What about target at pK contributes +1. Thus ΔL_gap = (# gap-changing ops) - (# target at p1 ops). Let O total gap-changing ops, z = number of target at p1 ops. Then ΔL_gap = O - z.

Is that always? What if target at p1 with K=1? Then no gaps, but K>=? B has at least one, K maybe1. If K=1, no gaps; operations with target at p1 keep piece, outside shifts move. We'll handle separately.

For K>1, yes: any gap-changing operation either target at p1 (L unchanged) or target elsewhere (L increases by1). Is there any operation target outside? no gap. So ΔL_gap = O - z, where z is count of operations choosing target equal to current leftmost piece? But if multiple pieces at leftmost? target at p1; if target at a position left of p1 is outside left, not gap-changing. If target at p1 and p1 maybe not unique, still L unchanged, R? If K>1 and target at leftmost, pieces to right move left, gap between first and second decreases by1; L unchanged. Good.

Then total t = O + |ΔL - (O - z)| = O + |(L_f - L_i) - O + z|.

This depends on O and z. z ≤ O. We can choose extra target-at-p1 operations (which reduce gap1 by1 each) to adjust translation. They also count in O and reduce gap1. But δ_1 may limit z? Actually target at p1 reduces gap1. We need reduce gap1 by δ_1; z cannot exceed δ_1? It could be more? If gap1 already 0, target at p1 would not reduce gap (no gap) and not change L? If p2>p1, gap1 positive; if p2=p1, then leftmost group has multiple, target at p1? Actually if p1=p2, target at p1, pieces at p1 stay, p3 moves left; gap between p2 and p3 decreases, not gap1. L unchanged. So target at p1 can reduce the first positive gap after a block of leftmost pieces, not necessarily gap1. Our simple gap indices with duplicates need care. But initially distinct; after operations, duplicates. However final q may have duplicates. The ordered gap vector h can have zeros. Gap reduction of a zero gap? If g_r=0, cannot reduce further; target at piece within a block may reduce adjacent positive gaps. We need handle duplicates robustly.

Maybe we can avoid detailed z by considering final q and operation count via another invariant.

Let's step back. Maybe there is a known formula for min operations using "potential" F(p) = sum over pieces something? Let's search memory.

Operation choose i, all pieces move one step closer. This is equivalent to applying the "clamp" operator to the multiset. The minimum number of operations to reach support B might be expressible as:
If possible, answer = max( max_{i}(dist(A_i, B?)), something like max over intervals of (number of A ones - number of B ones)? )

For p=[1,2,4], B={2,4}, answer 2. What lower bounds?
- Number pieces K=3, M=2. Need move? Maybe answer = max( min matching distance? 1, span reduction? ceil((3-2)/2)=1, gap? )

Let's compute operation count via gap reductions for p=[1,2,4] to q=[2,2,4]. Initial gaps g=[1,2], final h=[0,2]. δ=[1,0]. Need reduce gap1 by1. Gap-changing options to reduce gap1 by1:
- target at p1 (s=1): reduces gap1 by1, ΔL=0, O=1,z=1.

Now we need optimize over group sizes/cuts. Maybe we can compute minimal operations using gap reduction "cost" and outside shift.

Let's formalize for a chosen final q (group sizes). Let K pieces, initial gaps g_1..g_{K-1}. Let B positions s_1<...<s_M. Choose cuts 1 <= c_1 < c_2 < ... < c_{M-1} <= K-1? Actually groups: group m contains pieces from prev_cut+1 to next_cut. Let cut r_m be the gap between group m and m+1, i.e., last piece of group m = r_m, first of group m+1 = r_m+1. So 1 <= r_1 < ... < r_{M-1} <= K-1. Let r_0=0, r_M=K. Group m size = r_m - r_{m-1} >=1.
Final gaps h_r:
- if r = r_m for some m (boundary): h_r = D_m = s_{m+1}-s_m.
- otherwise (inside group): h_r = 0.
Reachability requires g_r >= D_m for boundary cuts; for inside, g_r>=0 automatically. So cuts must satisfy g_{r_m} >= D_m.

Then δ_r = g_r - h_r:
- boundary r_m: δ = g_{r_m} - D_m >=0.
- inside: δ = g_r.

Need reduce all gaps by δ_r.

Now, what is minimal number of gap-changing operations O and ΔL_gap for a given δ vector? We also need total t = O + |ΔL - ΔL_gap|, where ΔL = s_1 - p_1 (fixed). But ΔL_gap = O - z, where z = number of L-ops (target leftmost) among gap-changing operations. Thus t = O + |ΔL - O + z|. Since z between 0 and O. For fixed O, the minimal over z is? The expression O + |ΔL - O + z|. z can vary depending on schedule. We want minimize. Since z ≤ O and also z limited by something? Let's first ignore z constraints and find min over z∈[0,O] of O + |ΔL - O + z|. The absolute is minimized when z = O - ΔL if that lies in [0,O], giving t=O. If ΔL >=0, choose z=O-ΔL if O>=ΔL; if O<ΔL, choose z=0 giving O + (ΔL-O)=ΔL. If ΔL<0, choose z=O? then expression O + |ΔL - O + O| = O + |ΔL| = O - ΔL (since ΔL negative). Actually if ΔL<0, choose z=O gives O + |ΔL|. But z=O means all gap-changing ops are L-ops. Is that possible? Maybe not. So lower bound:
If ΔL >=0: t >= max(O, ΔL)? Let's compute:
- O >= ΔL: can choose z=O-ΔL, t=O.
- O < ΔL: choose z=0, t=ΔL.
So t >= max(O, ΔL).
If ΔL <0: t >= O - ΔL = O + |ΔL| (since z=O gives no outside shift? Actually U=ΔL - (O-z). If z=O, U=ΔL, outside left |ΔL|, t=O+|ΔL|). Could choose z<O gives larger. So t >= O - ΔL.
Thus for fixed O, minimal possible t (if z can be chosen appropriately) is:
- ΔL >=0: max(O, ΔL)
- ΔL <0: O - ΔL (since ΔL negative, O - ΔL = O + |ΔL| ≥ O).
But z constraints may force more.

Let's test p=[1,2,4], q=[2,2,4], ΔL=1. δ=[1,0]. Minimal O? Need reduce gap1 by1. Options:
- L-op: O=1, z=1. ΔL_gap=0, U=1, t=2. Formula ΔL>=0: max(O,ΔL)=max(1,1)=1, but actual2 because z=O=1 not O-ΔL=0; wait if ΔL=1,O=1, choose z=O-ΔL=0, but z=0 means no L-op; the only way to reduce gap1 with z=0 is target at piece2 internal, which would also reduce gap2 (δ2=0) impossible. So z constraint matters: for this δ, the only operation has z=1, so t=2.

Thus need account for z (L-ops) required by gap reduction schedule. z is number of operations targeting leftmost block. It is not arbitrary; it depends on how we reduce gaps. We need compute minimal O and possible z.

Maybe there is a dual: L-ops are the only gap-changing operations that do not increase L. They are necessary to reduce gaps near the left without shifting the whole configuration right. In formula, z = O - ΔL_gap. We can think of each gap-changing operation contributes either 0 (if L-op) or +1 to ΔL_gap. So z is number of "left-anchored" operations.

Can we compute minimal t directly as minimal over sequence of operations of O + |U|, where U = ΔL - (O-z). Since z is count of L-ops.

Maybe there is an invariant involving the leftmost piece and the first target? Let's consider the process in terms of "time" and "leftmost position L". L can change:
- L-op (target leftmost): L unchanged, reduces some gap from left.
- Other gap-changing op: L increases by1.
- Outside right: L increases by1.
- Outside left: L decreases by1.
Total L change ΔL = (#non-L gap ops) + (#outside right) - (#outside left). Let O_nonL = O - z. Then ΔL = O_nonL + a - b. Total t = O + a+b = z + O_nonL + a+b. Since a-b = ΔL - O_nonL. Minimal a+b = |ΔL - O_nonL|. So t = z + O_nonL + |ΔL - O_nonL|. This matches.

If ΔL >= O_nonL, t = z + ΔL. If ΔL < O_nonL, t = z + 2O_nonL - ΔL.

So for a given gap reduction schedule, O_nonL = number of gap-changing ops not targeting leftmost, z = number targeting leftmost. Need minimize.

Now, how to reduce gaps? This looks like we need to reduce each gap to target h. Operations can be classified as:
- L-op: reduces the first positive gap (leftmost) by1; z+1, O_nonL+0.
- R-op (target rightmost): reduces the last positive gap by1; O_nonL+1.
- Internal op at a piece/block: reduces one positive gap on left and one on right of a block? More generally, if we have a sequence of positive gaps separated by zero gaps, an internal target at a block can reduce the positive gaps immediately left and right of that block by1. This is like choosing a zero-run (block) and reducing the gaps on both sides. Between operation reduces a single gap by2 (if gap>=2) without affecting neighbors.

This is similar to reducing a histogram of gap heights to zero/target by operations that either:
- reduce the leftmost positive column by1 (L-op)
- reduce the rightmost positive column by1 (R-op)
- reduce two adjacent positive columns by1 (internal target at piece between them) — but if there are zero columns between, can reduce two positive columns separated by a zero block? Actually target at a block of pieces corresponds to a zero gap (or multiple consecutive zero gaps) between pieces; it reduces the positive gap immediately to the left of the block and immediately to the right of the block. In terms of positive gap indices, it reduces two positive gaps that are adjacent in the sequence after ignoring zero gaps? Let's define "blocks" of pieces at same position. Gaps between blocks are positive distances. A target at a block reduces the gaps to its neighboring blocks by1 (left and right), unless it's leftmost/rightmost block (then only one side). Thus operations act on the sequence of positive gaps between blocks: choose a block (not endpoint) and reduce the gaps on both sides by1; choose leftmost block reduce first gap by1 (L-op); choose rightmost block reduce last gap by1 (R-op); choose between two blocks (target strictly between) reduces that gap by2. This is exactly like we have a path graph of blocks, with edge weights = positive gaps. Operation:
- Choose an internal vertex (block): decrease the two incident edge weights by1.
- Choose left endpoint vertex: decrease first edge by1 (L-op).
- Choose right endpoint: decrease last edge by1 (R-op).
- Choose an edge (between two blocks): decrease that edge weight by2.
All weights must remain nonnegative. This is much clearer! Initially pieces are distinct, so blocks = K pieces, edges = gaps. After operations, blocks merge when edge weight becomes0. We can still choose a block (which may represent multiple original pieces) and decrease adjacent edge weights.

Final configuration has M blocks at B positions, with edge weights D_m between B positions. Internal edges (within a group of pieces assigned to same B square) have weight0 and correspond to merged blocks. So the process is: We have an initial path with K vertices (pieces) and edge lengths g_r. We need merge vertices into M groups, with final edge lengths between groups equal to D_m. Merging occurs when edge lengths reduce to0. We can reduce edge lengths by operations:
- At a vertex, reduce incident edge lengths by1 (if both exist; endpoints one).
- On an edge, reduce that edge length by2.
We need minimize operations + outside shifts.

This is like a graph editing problem on a path. Since final edge lengths are specified for M-1 edges (between groups), and all other edges 0. The initial path has K-1 edges. We choose M-1 edges to remain (not merged) with final lengths D_m; all other edges are reduced to0 (merged). Operations can reduce edges.

This resembles "minimum number of moves to transform edge weights" where each operation at a vertex reduces adjacent edges by1, or on edge reduces by2. Since edge reductions are independent but coupled by vertex operations.

Let's ignore outside shifts for a moment and compute minimal gap-changing operations O and z for a chosen set of surviving edges.

Let edges indexed 1..K-1. Choose surviving edges r_1<...<r_{M-1}, final lengths D_m. For each edge e:
required reduction δ_e = g_e - h_e (h_e = D_m if survivor else 0).

We need apply reductions to edges. Each operation:
- Vertex op at internal vertex v (between edge v-1 and v): reduces δ_{v-1} and δ_v by1 (if both positive; if one already target, can't overreduce). This is like choosing an internal vertex and decrementing both adjacent edge remaining reductions.
- Endpoint op at left vertex (edge1): reduces δ_1 by1; this is L-op (z).
- Endpoint op at right vertex (edge K-1): reduces δ_{K-1} by1; O_nonL.
- Edge op on edge e: reduces δ_e by2 (requires δ_e>=2 at time).

We need reduce all δ_e to0. This is exactly a "chip-firing" / "smoothing" problem. Since edge op reduces one edge by2, vertex op reduces two adjacent edges by1. Endpoint op reduces one edge by1.

This is equivalent to: We have a sequence of required reductions δ_e. z = left endpoint ops.

Thus within an interval, the only allowed operations are:
- vertex ops at internal vertices of the interval (reducing two adjacent positive edges)
- edge ops on a single edge (reducing by2)
- If the interval touches the global left endpoint (edge1), then left endpoint op is allowed (reduces edge1).
- If touches global right endpoint (edge K-1), right endpoint op allowed.
No other endpoint ops.

- Edge op on an edge reduces that edge by2.
- If interval includes edge1, left endpoint op reduces first edge by1.
- If interval includes edge K-1, right endpoint op reduces last edge by1.

This is equivalent to a path graph with L edges, where boundary vertices may have "external" zero edges; endpoint ops only at the two ends of the whole path, not at interval boundaries. So each interval is a path with optional boundary endpoint ops at its ends if they coincide with global ends.

If the leftmost component does not touch right and R odd, z must be1. If leftmost component R even, z=0. If there is no component containing edge1 (δ1=0), z=0. Thus possible z values:
- If leftmost component has odd R:
   * If it also touches right (component is entire path), z can be 0 or1.
   * Else z = 1.
- Else z = 0.
Additionally, if leftmost component has odd R and touches right, choosing z=1 vs0 changes O? O same (OddEndpoint=1). z=1 gives O_nonL=O-1; z=0 gives O_nonL=O. We can choose whichever minimizes t.

Now, we need choose final q (group sizes/cuts) to minimize t. The δ vector depends on cuts:
For each edge r:
- If r is a cut (boundary between groups), h_r = D_m (distance between B positions). δ_r = g_r - D_m.
- If not cut, h_r = 0. δ_r = g_r.

Reachability requires for cut r: g_r >= D_m. For non-cut, δ=g_r >0 (initial gaps positive). Thus δ_e is positive for all non-cut edges, and for cut edges δ may be zero if g_r = D_m, positive if >D_m. Since initial gaps positive, δ_e >=0. Zeros occur only at cut edges where g_r = D_m exactly. Also if g_r < D_m, cannot cut.

We need minimize t over choosing M-1 cut positions among K-1 satisfying g_r >= D_m. Note D_m are distances between consecutive B squares, positive. The order of cuts matters: cut r_m must be increasing and correspond to D_m in order. So we need select an increasing sequence r_1<...<r_{M-1} with g_{r_m} >= D_m. Non-cut edges have δ=g_r (positive). Cut edges have δ=g_r-D_m (could be zero). This is like matching B gaps to initial gaps in order, with each selected gap at least D_m.

This is a classic subsequence selection. K up to 1e6, M up to K. Need compute min t efficiently.

But maybe we can simplify further. Since non-cut δ = g_r, and cut δ = g_r - D_m. The total R = sum over all edges g_r - sum over cuts D_m = (p_K - p_1) - (s_M - s_1) = initial span - final span = S - H. This is fixed independent of cuts! Nice. Because sum g = p_K-p_1 = S; sum h = sum D_m = s_M-s_1 = H. Thus R = S-H is constant. Great. Maybe we can greedily assign groups from left to right: To maximize feasibility, for each B gap D_m, we need choose a cut r > previous cut with g_r >= D_m. Since only condition on individual g_r, not on cumulative sums. We can choose any gap large enough. To leave room for later groups, choose the earliest possible cut? For subsequence matching with thresholds, greedy earliest works for existence: scan gaps, when current required D_m <= g_r, take cut and move to next D. Because all D_m positive and order fixed; choosing earliest leaves more gaps for later. Yes, existence of cuts with g_r >= D_m in order can be checked greedily: for m=1..M-1, find the first r > previous cut with g_r >= D_m. If none, impossible. This is standard. Since we only need each selected gap at least D_m, no other constraints. So reachability (ignoring parity/internal odd components) is greedy.

Now, maybe the optimal t can be found by considering only the first and last positive components and ΔL. Let's derive t formula in terms of components more explicitly.

Let components C1..Cc. For each component j, length, sum R_j, parity p_j. Feasibility internal p_j=0. Let O_j = ceil(R_j/2) if j=1 or c (boundary) and p_j=1? Actually for any component:
- If p_j=0: O_j = R_j/2.
- If p_j=1:
   * if j=1 or j=c (boundary component): O_j = (R_j+1)/2.
   * if internal: infeasible.
So O = sum_j R_j/2 + (number of boundary odd components)/2 = R/2 + E/2, where E = p_1 + p_c (if c>1; if c=1, E=p_1). This matches.

Now A = O - z. z = p_1 if c>1? If c>1, first component is not last, and if p_1=1, left endpoint op needed, z=1; if p_1=0, z=0. If c=1, z can be 0 or1 if p_1=1, else0.

Thus A = sum_j O_j - z.
For first component:
- If c>1:
   A_1 = O_1 - p_1 = R_1/2 if p_1=0? If p_1=0, O=R/2, z=0 => A=R/2. If p_1=1, O=(R+1)/2, z=1 => A=(R-1)/2. So A_1 = floor(R_1/2)? Since if R1 even R/2, if odd (R-1)/2. Yes A_1 = floor(R_1/2).

   If p=1: z can be0 -> A=O=(R+1)/2; z=1 -> A=(R-1)/2. So A can be floor(R/2) or ceil(R/2).
For last component j=c:
- If c>1: no z effect, A_c = O_c = ceil(R_c/2) (if p_c=1) or R_c/2.
Internal components: A_j = R_j/2 (p=0).

So A = floor(R_1/2) + sum_{j=2}^{c-1} R_j/2 + ceil(R_c/2) for c>1, with internal feasible. For c=1, A ∈ {floor(R/2), ceil(R/2)} (if R odd; if even both equal R/2).

But R = sum R_j. Thus for c>1:
A = (R - R_c)/2? Let's compute: floor(R1/2) + internal R/2 + ceil(Rc/2). Since R1 and Rc parities p1,pc. floor(R1/2) = (R1 - p1)/2. ceil(Rc/2) = (Rc + pc)/2. Internal sum = (R - R1 - Rc)/2. So A = (R - p1 + pc)/2. Nice! For c>1. For c=1, A can be (R - p)/2 or (R + p)/2 where p=R mod2.

Then t = O + |ΔL - A|.

Let's express t for c>1:

A = (R - p1 + pc)/2.
Then ΔL - A = ΔL - (R - p1 + pc)/2.
t = (R + p1 + pc)/2 + |ΔL - (R - p1 + pc)/2|.

This depends only on R, p1, pc, ΔL! Not on internal component sums (as long feasible). Great. For c=1:

For c>1, p1 = parity of first component (touches left), pc = parity of last component (touches right). Feasibility requires internal components even, but their sums don't affect t except through R and p1,pc. Also p1,pc are determined by prefix parity at first/last positive edges:

Thus p1 in formulas should be parity of left boundary component if exists, else 0? But if no left boundary component, there is no component with odd sum allowed at left; the first component is internal and must be even. Similarly pc.

Let's define:

Rcomp exists iff δ_{K-1}>0. p_R = parity of last component. If no Rcomp, p_R=0.
If there is exactly one component and it touches both (δ all positive), then p_L=p_R=p, E=p (not 2p). If there are multiple components, left and right components are distinct if both exist; E = p_L + p_R. If only one boundary component exists (e.g., δ1>0 but δ_{K-1}=0, so left component exists, right none), then E = p_L (if odd) because it touches left; internal components must even. If no boundary components (δ1=0 and δ_{K-1}=0), all components internal must even, E=0, O=R/2, z=0.

Maybe general answer is max(Lshift, Rshift) + (some penalty from zero gaps/components). Let's derive t in terms of Lshift and Rshift for general δ.

Can we express t = max(L, Rsh) + penalty? Let's compute.

Let E ∈ {0,1,2}, z ∈ {0,1}. O = (L+Rsh+E)/2.
A = O - z = (L+Rsh+E)/2 - z.

Then t = O + |L - A| = (L+Rsh+E)/2 + | L - (L+Rsh+E)/2 + z |
= (L+Rsh+E)/2 + | (L - Rsh - E)/2 + z |.

Let D = L - Rsh. Then t = (R+E)/2 + | (D - E)/2 + z |. Since R=L+Rsh.

Multiply by 2? t integer. Let's analyze cases.

If E=0, z=0: t = R/2 + |D|/2 = (R + |D|)/2 = max(L,Rsh). Good.

If E=1:
- z can be? If E=1 means exactly one boundary component odd. z is 1 if that odd component is left boundary and not all-positive; if odd component is right boundary, z=0. O=(R+1)/2.
If z=1: A=O-1=(R-1)/2. t=O + |L - (R-1)/2|.
If z=0: t=O + |L - O|.
We can compute in terms of L,Rsh.
z=0: O=(R+1)/2. t = max(L, Rsh+1)? Earlier for all-positive z=0 gave max(L,Rsh+1).

- If odd component is right only: t = max(L, Rsh+1).
- If all-positive (odd component both): choose z to get t = max(L,Rsh).

If E=2: t = max(L,Rsh)+1.

- E=0: penalty0.
- E=1 with z optional (all-positive): penalty0.
- E=1 with z=1 forced (odd left only): penalty? t = max(L+1,Rsh). This equals max(L,Rsh) + 1 if L >= Rsh? If L<Rsh, max(L+1,Rsh) could be Rsh (if Rsh > L+1) no penalty. Example L=0,Rsh=3, t=max(1,3)=3=max. So penalty not always +1. It is max(L+1,Rsh).
- E=1 with z=0 forced (odd right only): t = max(L,Rsh+1).
- E=2: t = max(L+1,Rsh+1) = max(L,Rsh)+1.

So t depends on L,Rsh and whether odd boundary components are left/right.

Now, can we choose cuts to avoid E=2 or forced left/right odd? Since R = L+Rsh fixed. The parity of R = L+Rsh. E parity? E ≡ R mod2? Let's check: total R parity = sum component parities. - R even: E=0 or2. If E=0, t=max(L,Rsh). If E=2, t=max(L,Rsh)+1.
- R odd: E=1. If all-positive (single component touches both), t=max(L,Rsh). Else E=1 forced left or right, t=max(L+1,Rsh) or max(L,Rsh+1). Since L+Rsh=R odd, one of L,Rsh is larger.    * If R odd and not all-positive, t = max(L,Rsh)+1.
   * If R odd and all-positive, t = max(L,Rsh).

Great! This is much simpler.

   t_z1 = max(L+1, Rsh).

Thus for R odd with two boundary components (one odd one even), we can choose? The odd boundary side is determined by parities. If the odd boundary is on the side with larger shift, penalty0; if on smaller side, penalty1. If only one boundary component exists, the odd side is that side; same rule.

For R even, E=2 (both boundary components odd) gives t = max(L+1,Rsh+1) = M+1 always. E=0 gives M.

So the penalty is:
- If R even:
   * E=0 -> M
   * E=2 -> M+1
- If R odd:

   * not all-positive -> if the unique odd boundary component is on the side with larger shift, M; otherwise M+1.

Rsh=1 smaller. Thus to avoid penalty, put the odd endpoint on the smaller shift side? For L=1,Rsh=2, choose left endpoint z=1 gives max(2,2)=2; left is smaller. So yes, the odd endpoint should be on the smaller shift side. Intuitively, an endpoint op (which reduces gap by1) also shifts L? Left endpoint op doesn't shift L, right endpoint op shifts L by1. To minimize total, we want the extra parity operation on the side that needs less translation? Let's accept.

Thus for R odd with one boundary component odd:
- If the odd boundary is on the smaller shift side, penalty0.
- If on larger shift side, penalty1.
For all-positive, we can choose the odd endpoint to be on smaller shift side, so penalty0 always.

For R even E=2: both boundary components odd, so one is smaller one larger, but both odd, penalty1 always. If E=0 penalty0.

Can we choose cuts to avoid E=2 or place odd boundary appropriately? This is now the core optimization.

Let's define Lshift = L = s1 - p1, Rshift = Rsh = pK - sM. M0 = max(L,Rsh). R = L+Rsh.

We need select cuts satisfying g_r >= D_m. For each cut, δ_r = g_r - D_m. Non-cut δ=g>0. Zero δ only at cuts with equality.

We want minimize t = M0 + penalty, where penalty is 0 or1 depending on E and odd side, with feasibility.

Maybe the penalty can be determined by the parity of boundary components, which in turn depends on the sum of δ over prefixes. Let's express boundary component parity in terms of δ and zeros.

Let's denote:
- left boundary component exists iff δ1 > 0. Since edge1 is non-cut unless M? Could edge1 be a cut? Yes if first cut r1=1. Then δ1 = g1 - D1. If g1 > D1, δ1>0; if g1=D1, δ1=0. If edge1 is not cut, δ1=g1>0. So left boundary component exists iff either edge1 not cut, or cut with strict inequality. It does NOT exist iff edge1 is cut with equality.
Similarly right boundary component exists iff edge K-1 not cut or cut strict.

The parity of left boundary component (if exists) is the sum of δ over the first component, from edge1 up to before the first zero δ. If there are no zeros, it's total R parity. If there is a zero at position z (first zero), the left component sum = sum_{e=1}^{z-1} δ_e. Since δ_z=0. This is P_{z-1} mod2. If no zero, left component is entire (all-positive) if δ_{K-1}>0; if there are zeros? first zero exists.

Zeros occur only at cuts with equality. So the first zero position is the first cut (in edge order) with g_r = D_m. If no such cut, no zeros => all-positive (since non-cut positive, strict cuts positive). Thus all-positive iff no selected cut has equality. Great! So zeros are exactly perfect cuts.

If there is at least one perfect cut, the sequence of positive components is separated by perfect cuts. Boundary components:
- Left component exists iff edge1 is before the first perfect cut? Actually if first perfect cut is at position z, then edges 1..z-1 are positive (unless z=1, then no left component). Its sum parity = P_{z-1}.
- Right component exists iff last perfect cut is at position z, then edges z+1..K-1 positive; sum parity = P_{K-1} xor P_z.
- Internal components between perfect cuts must have even sum.

Thus the penalty depends on parities of the prefix sums before the first perfect cut and after the last perfect cut, and whether there are no perfect cuts.

Let's define perfect cuts set Z = {r_m | g_{r_m} = D_m}. If Z empty: all-positive, penalty0 (always feasible? Need cuts exist; yes if no equality, all δ positive, one component, no internal parity issue). Then answer = M0.

If Z nonempty:
Let z_first = min Z, z_last = max Z.
Left boundary component exists iff z_first > 1? Wait if first perfect cut is at edge z, then edges 1..z-1 are positive. If z=1, edge1 δ=0, so no left component. If z>1, left component exists. Its sum parity p_L = P_{z_first - 1} mod2 (sum δ_1..δ_{z_first-1}). But note δ_e for e<z_first are non-cut or strict cuts, all positive; P_{z_first-1} = (G_{z_first-1} - sum of D for strict cuts before z_first) mod2.
Right boundary component exists iff z_last < K-1. Its sum parity p_R = (P_{K-1} xor P_{z_last}) mod2 = sum δ_{z_last+1..K-1} mod2.
Internal components between perfect cuts: for each consecutive perfect cuts z_a < z_b, the component edges z_a+1..z_b-1 (if any) must have even sum. Since δ at perfect cuts are zero, the sum between them = P_{z_b-1} xor P_{z_a}. Need even. This is a feasibility condition on the set of perfect cuts.

But wait what about strict cuts (g>D) inside components? They have positive δ and don't split components. They contribute to sums and parities. So the perfect cuts are the only zeros.

Thus the problem reduces to selecting cuts (some strict, some perfect) satisfying g_r >= D_m, and then:
- If no perfect cuts: answer M0.
- If perfect cuts exist: need internal components between perfect cuts have even sum; compute p_L, p_R; penalty:
   * R even:
      - If p_L=1 and p_R=1 (both boundary components odd) => penalty1.
      - Else (both even, since R even and internal even) => penalty0.
      Note if no left component, p_L=0; if no right, p_R=0.
   * R odd:
      - If there is exactly one component? That would mean no perfect cuts, handled.
      - With perfect cuts, there is at least one zero, so not all-positive. The unique odd boundary component? Since R odd and internal even, exactly one of p_L,p_R is1 (if both boundary components exist) or one exists and odd. Penalty1 if the odd boundary is on the larger shift side? Wait earlier for R odd with not all-positive, penalty0 if odd boundary on smaller shift side. Let's express:
         If p_L=1 and p_R=0: odd side is left. t = max(L+1, Rsh). Penalty over M = max(L,Rsh) is 0 if Rsh > L? Let's check: t_z1 = max(L+1,Rsh). M=max(L,Rsh).
           - If L > Rsh: t=L+1=M+1 (penalty1)
           - If L < Rsh: t=Rsh=M (penalty0) (since Rsh >= L+1)
           - L=Rsh impossible R odd.
         So penalty0 iff L < Rsh (left shift smaller than right shift).
         If p_R=1 and p_L=0: t = max(L, Rsh+1). Penalty0 iff Rsh < L (right shift smaller).
         If both p_L=1 and p_R=1 with R odd impossible (E=2 even).
         If neither with R odd impossible.
      So penalty0 iff the odd boundary is on the side with smaller shift. If odd boundary on larger side, penalty1.
   * R even:
      - p_L=p_R=1 => penalty1.
      - p_L=p_R=0 => penalty0.
      (p_L=1,p_R=0 or vice versa impossible with R even and internal even? Let's check total parity: R even = p_L xor p_R xor internal. Internal even, so p_L xor p_R=0 => p_L=p_R. So yes.)

Thus penalty is 0 or1. We need determine if there exists a valid cut selection with penalty0 (or minimal). Since answer is M0 + penalty, and penalty ∈ {0,1}, we just need check if penalty0 achievable. If yes answer M0, else M0+1 (provided reachable). Wait is penalty ever >1? E max2, so penalty at most1. Great! This is huge. So minimum operations is either max(Lshift,Rshift) or max+1, depending on parity/perfect cuts. Let's test all examples:

So for R odd, penalty0 if the unique odd boundary component is on the side with smaller shift. Since smaller shift side is:
- if L < Rsh: left side is smaller -> need p_L=1 (left odd)
- if Rsh < L: right side is smaller -> need p_R=1 (right odd)
- if L=Rsh impossible R odd.
If the odd boundary is on larger side, penalty1.

For R even, penalty0 iff p_L=p_R=0 (no odd boundary components). If p_L=p_R=1, penalty1.

Now, can we always achieve penalty0 by choosing cuts appropriately? Need check. If not, answer M0+1. But maybe if reachable, penalty0 is always achievable? Counterexample p=[1,2,4], B={2,4}: only valid cut perfect, left odd, left shift larger -> penalty1, so answer M0+1. Thus not always.

We need compute whether there exists a valid cut selection with penalty0. Since penalty only 0/1, answer is M0 if exists penalty0, else M0+1. But wait if no valid cut selection at all, impossible -1. Also need K>=M and span condition? Greedy will detect.

Now we need solve existence of cuts with penalty0. This is a combinatorial subsequence selection problem with parity constraints around perfect cuts.

Let's formalize.

Inputs:
- Initial piece positions p_1<...<p_K (from A ones). Gaps g_i = p_{i+1}-p_i for i=1..K-1.
- B positions s_1<...<s_M. Distances D_m = s_{m+1}-s_m for m=1..M-1.
Need choose cuts 1 <= r_1 < ... < r_{M-1} <= K-1 such that g_{r_m} >= D_m.

Let L = s_1 - p_1, Rsh = p_K - s_M. If L<0 or Rsh<0? Since final span <= initial span but B could start before initial and end before? L can be negative if s1 < p1, Rsh positive larger. Both can be negative? If s1 < p1 and sM > pK? Span final > initial impossible because R = L+Rsh = S-H >=0, but L negative, Rsh could be >R. Rsh = pK-sM could be negative if sM > pK, then L = R - Rsh > R positive. So one of L,Rsh can be negative? Wait L = s1-p1. If B shifted right, L positive, Rsh maybe negative? Example initial p=[5,6], B=[6,7]: L=1, Rsh=5-7=-2, R = L+Rsh = -1? But span final=1, initial=1, R=0? Actually S=1,H=1,R=0, L=1,Rsh=-1? pK=6,sM=7 => -1, sum0. So Rsh can be negative! Earlier I assumed Rsh = pK-sM >=0? Not necessarily; final support can extend beyond initial right while also start to right, span same. Since span non-increasing, if B span <= initial span, it can be shifted right so sM > pK, then Rsh negative. Similarly L can be negative. R = L+Rsh = S-H >=0, but L and Rsh individually can be negative. The max M0 = max(L,Rsh) could be negative? If both negative? That would mean s1<p1 and sM>pK, final span > initial? Since R=L+Rsh = (s1-p1)+(pK-sM) = S-H >=0. If both negative, R<0, impossible. So at least one nonnegative. M0 = max(L,Rsh) may be negative? If R=0 and L=Rsh=0? If R=0, L=-Rsh. Could L negative, Rsh positive. Max nonnegative. So M0 >=0.

Need be careful with formulas involving L,Rsh when negative. The derivation t = max(L,Rsh) for all-positive used L and Rsh as shifts; if one negative, max still works? Example p=[5,6], B=[6,7] (M=2, K=2, g=1,D=1 perfect δ=0,R=0,L=1,Rsh=-1). No gap ops, need move both right by1: answer1. max(1,-1)=1. Good. If L negative, Rsh positive, max positive. So M0 = max(L,Rsh) is the required outside translation? It is at least0.

Our penalty rules with L<Rsh etc. still work with negative values? Need test. Suppose L=-1,Rsh=2,R=1. M=2. R odd. If odd boundary left? t_z1=max(L+1=0,Rsh=2)=2=M penalty0? left shift smaller (-1<2) yes. If odd boundary right? t_z0=max(L=-1,Rsh+1=3)=3=M+1. So rule "odd on smaller shift side" works (left smaller). Good.

Now, how to check existence of penalty0 cut selection?

First, existence of any valid cuts can be checked greedily. But we also need parity constraints. Since M up to 1e6, we can perhaps do DP with states representing parity of current component and whether we've seen a perfect cut? The penalty0 condition depends on p_L and p_R, which are parities of boundary components separated by perfect cuts. Internal components between perfect cuts must be even.

This is reminiscent of selecting a subsequence of cuts with certain "good" perfect cuts. Maybe we can transform by considering parities of prefix sums of g and D.

Let's define for each edge i:
- g_i (positive integer).
- If selected as cut for D_m, δ_i = g_i - D_m. Its parity is g_i mod2 xor D_m mod2.
- If not selected, δ_i = g_i.

We need choose cuts. The prefix parity P_e = (sum_{i=1}^e δ_i) mod2. Since δ_i = g_i - (D_m if cut else 0), P_e = G_e - C_e mod2, where G_e = sum_{i=1}^e g_i mod2, C_e = sum of D_m for selected cuts <= e mod2.

A perfect cut at r has δ_r=0, so P_r = P_{r-1}. For any cut (strict or perfect), δ_r may be positive but contributes to P.

Components of positive δ are separated by perfect cuts. Internal component between perfect cuts at positions a<b (with no perfect cuts between) has sum parity = P_{b-1} xor P_a (since δ_a=0, δ_b=0, component edges a+1..b-1). Need even: P_{b-1} = P_a.

Left boundary component before first perfect cut z: sum parity p_L = P_{z-1} (since P_0=0). If no left component (z=1), p_L=0? Actually if z=1, P_{0}=0, p_L=0 (no component). Formula p_L = P_{z-1} works (P_0=0).
Right boundary component after last perfect cut z: sum parity p_R = P_{K-1} xor P_z. If z=K-1, p_R=0.

Thus if perfect cuts set Z nonempty, internal even condition is: for consecutive perfect cuts a<b, P_{b-1} = P_a. This is equivalent to P_{a} = P_{b-1}. Since P_{b} = P_{b-1} (δ_b=0), so P_a = P_b. Thus the prefix parity P is constant across the zero at b? Actually P_b = P_{b-1}. So condition P_{b-1}=P_a means P_b = P_a. Therefore all perfect cuts (except maybe first?) have the same P value? Let's see: For first perfect cut z1, no left internal condition. For next z2, need P_{z2} = P_{z1} (since P_{z2}=P_{z2-1}). For z3, need P_{z3}=P_{z2}. Thus all perfect cuts after the first must have the same P value as the first. Wait what about component between z1 and z2: sum edges z1+1..z2-1 parity = P_{z2-1} xor P_{z1} = P_{z2} xor P_{z1} (because δ_{z2}=0 => P_{z2}=P_{z2-1}). Need 0 => P_{z2}=P_{z1}. Yes. So all perfect cuts must have the same prefix parity P! Let's verify with δ=[1,0,1]: perfect cut at edge2. Only one perfect cut, no condition. P_2? δ=[1,0], P_2=1. p_L=P_1? z=2, p_L=P_1=1; p_R=P_3 xor P_2 = (1+0+1=0? R=2 even P3=0) xor1=1. Good. If multiple perfect cuts, e.g., δ=[1,0,0,1]? Perfect cuts at2,3. Need component between empty, condition P_3=P_2? δ2=0,δ3=0, P2=1,P3=1 ok. If P differs, internal component odd infeasible.

So internal feasibility iff all perfect cuts have the same prefix parity value Q = P_{r} for every perfect cut r. Since P_r = P_{r-1} for perfect cut. Let's call this Q ∈ {0,1}. Then p_L = P_{z1-1} = P_{z1} (because perfect) = Q. Wait if z1 is first perfect cut, P_{z1-1}=P_{z1}=Q. So p_L = Q if left component exists? If z1=1, P_0=0, but P_1=0 because δ1=0, so Q=0, p_L=0. Formula P_{z1-1}=P_0=0=Q. Good. p_R = P_{K-1} xor P_{z_last} = R mod2 xor Q. Since P_{K-1}=R mod2. Thus:
- p_L = Q.
- p_R = R_mod xor Q.
Where R_mod = R mod2.

This is elegant! For any feasible perfect cut set, all perfect cuts have the same prefix parity Q, and boundary parities are p_L=Q, p_R=R_mod xor Q.

If there are no perfect cuts, all-positive, penalty0.

If perfect cuts exist and feasible, penalty depends on Q and R_mod:
- R even (R_mod=0): p_L=Q, p_R=Q. Penalty0 iff Q=0 (both even); penalty1 iff Q=1 (both odd).
- R odd (R_mod=1): p_L=Q, p_R=1 xor Q. Exactly one boundary odd. Penalty0 iff the odd boundary is on the smaller shift side.
   * If Q=1 (left odd, right even): need left shift smaller: L < Rsh? (since left odd penalty0 iff Rsh > L).
   * If Q=0 (left even, right odd): need right shift smaller: Rsh < L.
   (If L=Rsh impossible R odd.)

So penalty0 condition reduces to:
- If no perfect cuts: penalty0.
- If perfect cuts exist:
   * Need all perfect cuts have same prefix parity Q (feasibility).
   * If R even: Q=0.
   * If R odd:
       - Q=1 and L < Rsh, or
       - Q=0 and Rsh < L.

But wait what if perfect cuts exist but there is no left/right boundary component? The formulas p_L=Q, p_R=R_mod xor Q still hold? If first perfect cut at edge1, Q=P_1=0 (since δ1=0), so p_L=0. If last perfect cut at K-1, P_{K-1}=Q? Since δ_{K-1}=0, P_{K-1}=P_{K-2}; but R_mod = P_{K-1}=Q. Then p_R=R_mod xor Q=0. Good. If perfect cuts at both boundaries and R even Q=0, p_L=p_R=0 penalty0. Example δ=[0,0] R=0, perfect cuts both edges? K=3, all δ zero, no positive components, penalty0. Good.

Thus the problem becomes: Does there exist a valid selection of cuts such that either:
(A) no selected cut is perfect (strict cuts only), or
(B) all perfect cuts have prefix parity Q satisfying the above penalty0 condition.

If yes answer M0; else if any valid selection exists answer M0+1; else -1.

But wait if there are no perfect cuts, all δ positive, one component, penalty0 regardless of R parity. So if we can choose all cuts strict (g_r > D_m), answer M0. This is often possible if for every D_m there is a gap > D_m in order. If not, some perfect cuts forced.

Now, how to check existence of valid cut selection with a given property efficiently?

First, existence of any valid cut selection: greedy scan gaps with required D_m. Since cuts must be in order and g_r >= D_m. Greedy earliest works. If fails, -1.

Now, property A (all cuts strict): Need select cuts with g_r > D_m for all m. This is same greedy with strict inequality. If succeeds, answer M0.

If property A fails, we need property B. But maybe property B can be checked by considering perfect cuts. A perfect cut at r for D_m means g_r = D_m. Since D_m are distances between B positions. Perfect cuts are forced when the only available gap for a D_m equals D_m. We need select cuts (some strict, some perfect) satisfying g_r >= D_m, and all perfect cuts have same prefix parity Q with condition.

This is a more complex subsequence selection with parity constraints on perfect cuts. However, note that if property A fails, any valid selection must use at least one perfect cut. We need determine if there exists a selection with perfect cuts all having Q (0 or1) satisfying condition. Since Q is just 0 or1, we can try both Q values and check if there is a valid cut selection whose perfect cuts all have prefix parity Q (and at least one perfect cut if Q condition? Actually if Q condition requires perfect cuts exist; but if no perfect cuts, property A already handled. We can just check existence with perfect cuts all Q, allowing zero perfect cuts? If zero perfect cuts, property A would have succeeded. So for property B, require at least one perfect cut, but if we check with Q and allow zero, property A covers. We can check existence of valid selection where every perfect cut has prefix parity Q. Strict cuts can be anything (including g>D). Need at least one perfect cut maybe, but if the check succeeds with zero perfect cuts, property A would also succeed, so answer M0 anyway. Thus we can just check existence of valid selection with all perfect cuts having prefix parity Q; if yes answer M0. But if property A fails, any successful selection must have at least one perfect cut, so okay.

Now, how to check existence of valid cut selection with constraint on perfect cuts' prefix parity Q?

Let's define for each possible cut r (edge index) and D_m:
- It is valid if g_r >= D_m.
- It is perfect if g_r == D_m.
- Its prefix parity if selected as cut: P_r = (G_r - C_r) mod2, where C_r = sum of D for selected cuts up to r mod2. This depends on previous selected cuts, not just r. The condition "perfect cut has prefix parity Q" means when we select a perfect cut at r as the m-th cut, the current prefix parity P_r must equal Q. But P_r = G_r mod2 xor (sum D of cuts up to r mod2). The sum D up to r includes this cut's D_m and previous cuts. So it depends on parity of previous D's.

This is like a DP over cuts with state = current prefix parity? But M up to 1e6, K up to1e6; O(K*M) impossible. Need exploit that we only need existence, not optimize, and the constraint is only on perfect cuts (rare? Could be many).

Maybe we can use greedy with parity state. Since cuts must be in order and each D_m matched to a gap. We can process gaps left to right, maintaining how many B gaps matched. At each step, we need match next D_m to some gap r > prev. The choice affects prefix parity P_r. For a perfect cut, we require P_r = Q. For strict cuts, no constraint on P_r (but P_r changes).

We need know if there exists a sequence of cuts. This is a regular language over matching D_m to gaps, with state = parity of C (sum D of cuts so far) maybe. Since P_r = G_r xor C. For a perfect cut at r, require G_r xor C = Q, i.e., C = G_r xor Q. C is the parity of sum of D_m of selected cuts so far (including current). Before selecting current cut, let C_prev be parity of D_1..D_{m-1}. After selecting D_m, C_new = C_prev xor D_m_parity. For a perfect cut, require C_new = G_r xor Q. Thus C_prev = G_r xor Q xor D_m_parity.

For strict cuts, no constraint, but C_new updates.

We also need internal feasibility? We derived all perfect cuts same Q is sufficient for internal components even. Does the DP with perfect cuts all Q automatically ensure internal components even? Yes if all perfect cuts have P=Q. But wait if there are no perfect cuts, property A. If perfect cuts all have same P=Q, internal components even. So no additional constraint.

Thus we can model matching D_m to gaps with state c = parity of sum of D of cuts selected so far (C_prev before next cut). Initially c=0. For each required D_m (m from1 to M-1), we need choose a gap r > prev. After choosing, c ^= (D_m & 1). If the chosen gap is perfect (g_r == D_m), we additionally require that the prefix parity at r after selection equals Q, i.e., c_new == (G_r & 1) ^ Q. Since c_new = c ^ (D_m&1). Equivalently c == (G_r &1) ^ Q ^ (D_m&1). For strict cuts, no requirement.

We need know if there exists a sequence of M-1 gaps in increasing order satisfying these constraints and g_r >= D_m.

This is a subsequence matching with a small state c (0/1) and a condition on perfect cuts. We can solve greedily? Since we only need existence, and state is binary, we can maintain the set of possible (position, c) after matching each D_m. But positions are many; however we can maintain for each state the earliest position where we can be after matching m cuts. Because for subsequence matching, earlier position is always better (leaves more room for future). With two states, we can maintain earliest prev index for each c. Transition for next D: from state c at position prev, find the earliest gap r > prev with g_r >= D and, if perfect, satisfying condition. Then new state c' = c ^ (D&1), new position r. We want earliest r for each c'. Since state space 2, we can compute greedily.

This is promising! We can process D_m in order, maintaining dp[c] = minimum index of last cut after matching some number of cuts, with current parity c. Initially before any cuts, dp[0]=0, dp[1]=inf (no cuts, parity0). For each D:
- For each c with dp[c] finite, we need find the smallest r > dp[c] such that g_r >= D and (if g_r == D then (c ^ (D&1)) == (G_r&1) ^ Q else true).
- Then ndp[c ^ (D&1)] = min(ndp[...], r).
After processing all M-1 cuts, if any dp state finite, selection exists.

But is earliest position for each state sufficient? Yes, because future only depends on current parity and last cut position; smaller last cut is always at least as good (more remaining gaps). For strict cuts, any r works; for perfect cuts, condition depends on r and c. We need find earliest valid r > prev. This can be done with precomputed next arrays.

However, we need do this for Q=0 and Q=1 (and maybe property A). Complexity O(M) per Q, total O(M) if we can query earliest valid r quickly. M up to1e6, two Q's okay.

But wait, the parity c is C_prev = sum of D of cuts selected so far mod2. Initially 0. After each cut, c ^= D_m mod2. This is independent of Q. For perfect cut condition, we used P_r = G_r xor c_new = Q. Let's verify with sample1 δ=[1,1,1] no perfect cuts (property A) answer M0. If property A fails, Q check.

Let's test DP on p=[1,2,4], B={2,4}: K=2? Actually K=3? p=[1,2,4], B positions2,4, M=2, D=[2]. Gaps g=[1,2]. L=1,Rsh=0,R=1 odd. Property A strict g>D: need gap >2 none -> fails. Perfect cut at r=2 (g=2). Q condition for R odd: L=1,Rsh=0, L<Rsh? 1<0 false; Rsh<L? 0<1 true. Need Q=0 (right odd) for penalty0? Let's compute R odd, condition: Q=1 and L<Rsh? no; Q=0 and Rsh<L? yes. So Q=0 should yield penalty0? But actual answer is M+1=2, penalty1. Wait contradiction! Let's recalc p=[1,2,4], B={2,4}: L=s1-p1=2-1=1, Rsh=pK-sM=4-4=0. R=1 odd. Perfect cut r=2, g2=2,D=2. δ=[1,0]. R odd, p_L=1 (left odd), p_R=0. Q = P_r for perfect cut r=2: δ1=1, δ2=0, P_2=1. So Q=1, not 0. Our condition for penalty0: Q=1 and L<Rsh? 1<0 false; Q=0 and Rsh<L? false. So Q=1 gives penalty1. Good. DP with Q=1 should find selection (perfect cut) but penalty1; Q=0 should fail (because perfect cut P=1 not0). Since property A fails and Q=1 is the only perfect selection, answer M0+1. Great.

Now, if DP for Q=0 succeeds, answer M0. But we must also ensure that if property A fails and Q selection succeeds, there is at least one perfect cut. Could DP with Q succeed using only strict cuts? If property A fails, no all-strict selection, so any successful selection must include a perfect cut. But our DP allows strict cuts without condition; if it finds a selection with no perfect cuts, that would be property A, contradiction. Since property A failed, any successful sequence must include a perfect cut, so okay. If property A succeeded, we answer M0 without DP. Thus we can just run DP for Q values maybe only when needed.

But wait: What if property A fails, but DP for Q succeeds with a selection that has perfect cuts all Q, but there are internal components? We argued all perfect cuts same Q ensures internal even. Let's verify with a case of two perfect cuts with same Q but internal component even. Suppose δ=[1,0,1,0,1]? Perfect cuts at2,4. Need P_2 = P_4? δ1=1,δ2=0 => P2=1; δ3=1,δ4=0 => P4=0? P4 = P2 + δ3 + δ4 =1+1+0=0 mod2, not same. Internal component between cuts edges3 sum δ3=1 odd, infeasible. Our DP condition all perfect cuts have P=Q would require P2=P4, so this selection would not be allowed. Good.

Now, how to implement DP efficiently.

We have K-1 gaps g[1..K-1] (1-indexed). Need for each D_m (m=1..M-1) find earliest r > prev with g[r] >= D and (if g[r]==D then condition on G[r] and c).

We can precompute for each D? D values are distances between B positions, up to N. K up to1e6. We need query next position with g>=D, and among those maybe next perfect with condition. Since D changes, we can precompute sorted gaps? But we need positions > prev. We can use segment tree / sparse table for range minimum? Actually to find first index > prev with g[i] >= D, we can build a segment tree over gaps storing max, and query first position in suffix with max >= D in O(log N). Total O(M log N) per Q, M up to1e6, log ~20, two Q -> 40e6, okay in Python maybe borderline but acceptable with optimized segment tree? Sum N 1e6, T up to2e5, need efficient.

But we also need perfect condition: if the first gap >=D is strict (g>D), it's valid regardless of Q. If it's perfect, need check condition; if condition fails, we cannot choose that perfect cut, but maybe a later strict gap or a later perfect gap with different G? Wait for a given D_m, if the earliest valid gap is perfect and condition fails, we might skip it and choose a later gap (strict or perfect satisfying condition). The DP needs the earliest valid r satisfying the condition for the current state c. This is not just first g>=D; if first is perfect and fails, need continue searching. Could be many perfect gaps with same D? g values can repeat. Need find earliest r > prev such that (g[r] > D) OR (g[r]==D AND (c ^ Dparity) == G[r]^Q).

Let's define for a given D and state c, a gap r is allowed if:
- g[r] >= D
- if g[r] == D, then G[r] == (c ^ Dparity) ^ Q (mod2).
If g[r] > D, always allowed.

We need find first r > prev satisfying this.

We can precompute for each possible D? D up to N, but number of distinct D <= M. Need efficient query.

Option: Use segment tree storing for each node the maximum g and maybe information about perfect conditions. Since condition depends on parity G[r] and c/Q, we can precompute for each position r with value g[r]:
- If we consider it as a perfect match for D = g[r], its allowed parity condition is G[r] == target, where target = c ^ Dparity ^ Q. Since Dparity = g[r]&1. So for a perfect gap, the condition depends on c and Q: allowed if (G[r] &1) == (c ^ (g[r]&1) ^ Q). Equivalently c == (G[r]&1) ^ (g[r]&1) ^ Q. Let key[r] = (G[r]&1) ^ (g[r]&1). Then perfect gap r is allowed for state c if key[r] == c ^ Q? Actually c ^ Dparity ^ Q = G[r] => c = G[r] ^ Dparity ^ Q = key[r] ^ Q. So allowed if c == key[r] ^ Q.

For strict gaps (g[r] > D), always allowed. Note a gap can be strict for a smaller D and perfect for D equal to its value.

We need query first r > prev with either g[r] > D, or (g[r] == D and key[r] == c ^ Q).

This is like a 2D condition. Since D is the required value. We can process queries offline? For each D_m in order, prev increases. We need dynamic suffix queries. Could use segment tree where each node stores:
- max_g: maximum g in segment.
- For perfect condition, we need know if there exists a position with g == D and key == target. But D varies.

Maybe simpler: Because M up to1e6, we can precompute for each position the next strict gap > D? Not for all D.

Alternative: We can avoid DP over positions by using greedy with "earliest possible" and if perfect condition fails, maybe choose next strict. We can find next strict >D and next perfect with condition >prev, take min. Need data structures for:
1. next position > prev with g[i] > D.
2. next position > prev with g[i] == D and key[i] == target.

For (1), segment tree max query as before but condition >D (strict). For (2), we need positions by value and key. We can precompute lists of positions for each gap value and key? g values up to N, key 0/1. For each value v, we have two sorted lists of positions with g[i]==v and key[i]==0/1. Then next perfect with g==D and key==target is binary search in list[D][target] for >prev. Total O(log occurrences). Good.

For (1), we can also precompute for each D? Need first position >prev with g>D. Segment tree max can find first index in suffix with max > D. O(log K). We can build a segment tree over g storing max. Query first_gt(prev, D): find smallest r>prev with g[r] > D. Standard recursive/iterative segment tree.

Alternatively, since we process D_m in order and prev increases, we could use a segment tree with max and update? No updates. We can build a sparse table and binary search? To find first >prev with max >D, we can binary search on segment tree. O(log K).

For (2), lists by value and key.

Then for each state c, candidate r = min( first_gt(prev, D), first_perfect(prev, D, target=c^Q) ). If none, state infeasible. Then ndp[c^Dparity] = min(ndp, r). Since there are only two states, we can process.

But wait: If first_gt returns a position that is before a perfect candidate? We take min. If first_gt is allowed. If first_perfect is allowed. Good. What if a gap with g>D but key condition? Strict no condition. If a gap with g==D and key target allowed. If a gap with g<D not allowed. So min of the two firsts is earliest allowed.

Need also ensure r > prev. first_gt uses >prev. first_perfect uses >prev.

Now, is the DP with earliest position per state sufficient when perfect condition depends on key and state? Yes, because for a given state c, choosing the earliest allowed r is always optimal for future. It might lead to a different c? c' determined by Dparity, same for any r. So earliest r dominates later r for same c'. Thus we can keep dp[c] = minimal last cut position for parity c after processing current number of cuts. Initially dp[0]=0, dp[1]=INF. For each D:
- ndp = [INF, INF]
- For c in 0,1 if dp[c]<INF:
    target_perfect = c ^ Q  (since key == c ^ Q)
    r1 = first_gt(dp[c], D)
    r2 = first_perfect(dp[c], D, target_perfect)
    r = min(r1,r2)
    if r < INF:
       nc = c ^ (D & 1)
       ndp[nc] = min(ndp[nc], r)
- dp = ndp
After all cuts, if any dp finite, selection exists.

But wait: The parity c in DP is C_prev = sum of D of cuts selected so far mod2. Initially 0. After selecting a cut with D, c_new = c ^ (D&1). This is correct. For a perfect cut, condition P_r = Q. P_r = G_r mod2 xor c_new. So G_r xor c_new = Q => G_r = c_new xor Q. Since c_new = c ^ Dparity, target for key: key = G_r ^ Dparity = c ^ Q. Yes.

Now, first_gt should find r with g[r] > D (strict). first_perfect should find r with g[r] == D and key[r] == c ^ Q. But what about a gap with g[r] == D and key target, but this gap is also the first_gt? No, g==D not >D. So disjoint.

If g[r] > D, it is a strict cut. If g[r] == D and key target, perfect cut. If g[r] > D but we might prefer a later perfect cut for parity? Since strict cut has no condition and updates c in same way (c ^ Dparity), choosing earliest strict is always at least as good for future. Could choosing a later perfect cut instead of an earlier strict cut ever help? The only difference is the position (later is worse) and the fact that perfect cut imposes condition but if allowed, both update c same. Earlier position dominates. So min is fine.

Now, property A (all strict) can be checked by a simple greedy: scan gaps, for each D find first gap > D. This is equivalent to DP with no perfect allowed? We can just run a greedy: prev=0; for each D, find first r>prev with g[r] > D; if none fail. We can use first_gt. If succeeds, answer M0. Actually if all cuts strict, no perfect cuts, penalty0. So we can first check this. But note: If a gap g == D but there is a later strict gap, property A can choose later strict. Greedy earliest strict works.

If property A fails, we run DP for Q values? We need determine which Q gives penalty0. For R odd, Q must be 0 or1 depending on L,Rsh. For R even, Q must be0. But if Q condition says penalty0, we need check existence of valid selection with perfect cuts all Q. We can run DP for that specific Q. If succeeds, answer M0. If not, answer M0+1 (provided any valid selection exists). But wait, what if property A fails and DP for the required Q fails, but there is a valid selection with perfect cuts all Q but no perfect? impossible; property A fail. So no penalty0. Then answer M0+1 if any valid selection exists. We need check any valid selection exists (with g>=D, allowing perfect/strict) separately. We can check by greedy first_ge (>=D). If that fails, -1. Note property A may fail but first_ge may succeed.

But is it possible that property A fails, required Q DP fails, but there is a valid selection with perfect cuts not all same Q but still penalty0 due to no internal odd? No, internal feasibility requires all perfect cuts same Q. If perfect cuts not same Q, some internal component odd, impossible. So no.

What about a valid selection with no perfect cuts? Property A would succeed. So if property A fails, any valid selection has at least one perfect cut and must satisfy same Q.

Thus algorithm:
1. Parse A, get piece positions p list length K. Parse B, get positions s list length M.
2. If K < M: print(-1). (Also if no? constraints A,B have at least one one.)
3. Compute L = s[0] - p[0], Rsh = p[-1] - s[-1], R = (p[-1]-p[0]) - (s[-1]-s[0]) = L + Rsh. If R < 0? Since final span > initial span impossible; but if K>=M and gaps? Actually span condition necessary: s[-1]-s[0] <= p[-1]-p[0]. If not, no valid cuts? Since sum D > sum g, cannot select gaps with g>=D? Maybe possible? Sum of selected gaps <= sum all gaps = S, but sum D=H. If H>S, impossible. So check R>=0. If R<0 print(-1).
4. Build gaps g[i] = p[i+1]-p[i] for i=0..K-2 (0-index). Length G = K-1.
5. If M == 1: no cuts. Final all pieces at s[0]. Need min operations? Our formula with no cuts? If M=1, final support single square s1. There are no B distances. We need move all pieces to s1. What is min operations? Earlier K=2 p=[2,5], B={1}: answer4. L=s1-p1, Rsh=pK-s1, R = S - 0 = pK-p1 = L+Rsh. No cuts (M-1=0), δ = all gaps g (non-cut), all-positive (no zeros), answer M0 = max(L,Rsh)? Segment tree is fine. Let's design an efficient iterative segment tree.

Build size = 1; while size < G: size*=2. tree = [0]*(2*size); fill leaves tree[size+i]=g[i]; for i in range(size-1,0,-1): tree[i]=max(tree[2i],tree[2i+1]).

To find first index >= l (0-based) with value >= x:

    if l >= G: return -1
    # We'll search in the segment tree.
    # AtCoder max_right: find max r such that predicate on [l,r) true? We need first where max >= x.
    # Use function max_right(l, f) where f(max) is false until threshold.
    # Simpler: use a stack of segments covering [l,G).
We can implement:
def find_first(l, x, strict):
    # returns smallest index >= l with g[index] > x if strict else >= x
    if l >= G: return -1
    l += size
    # We need to consider nodes covering [l, size) but not beyond G.
    # We can do a recursive descent on the segment tree:
    # First, move l up to cover the suffix. Collect nodes in left-to-right order.
    # Standard:
    sm = -1? Not needed.
    # Use iterative segment tree "max_right" from AtCoder:
    # We want first position p >= l such that max(g[l:p+1]) satisfies threshold.
    # AtCoder max_right returns max r such that predicate holds for all elements in [l,r). Predicate: max < x (for ge) or max <= x (for gt). Then first violating is r.
    # We can implement max_right with a custom predicate on node max.
    # AtCoder max_right(l, f):
    #   if l == n: return n
    #   l += size
    #   sm = e (identity, for max e = -inf)
    #   while True:
    #       while l % 2 == 0: l //= 2

    # Need ensure not include beyond G; AtCoder max_right works with n=G and size power of two; leaves beyond G should be -1 so they don't affect max.
    # Set leaves beyond G to -1.

        def ok(v): return v <= x
    else:
        def ok(v): return v < x
    # But defining nested function per query is slow. We can implement two separate functions or pass mode and use if inside.
    # We'll implement a function with while loops and inline condition.
```
AtCoder max_right returns first index where predicate fails. For ge, predicate "max < x" holds for segments with all values < x. First fail is first value >= x. For gt, predicate "max <= x" holds for all values <= x. First fail is first value > x. Perfect.

Let's write max_right(l, x, strict):
```
if l >= G: return G
l += size
sm = -1  # max identity
while True:
    while l % 2 == 0:
        l //= 2
    if strict:
        if max(sm, tree[l]) > x:  # predicate fails? Wait predicate is max <= x. If max(sm, tree[l]) <= x, ok; if > x, fail.
            # descend
            while l < size:
                l *= 2
                if max(sm, tree[l]) <= x:
                    sm = max(sm, tree[l])
                    l += 1
            return l - size
    else:
        if max(sm, tree[l]) >= x:  # predicate max < x fails if >=x
            while l < size:
                l *= 2
                if max(sm, tree[l]) < x:
                    sm = max(sm, tree[l])
                    l += 1
            return l - size
    sm = max(sm, tree[l])
    l += 1
    if (l & -l) == l:
        break
return G
```
Need check AtCoder max_right logic. Let's test mentally. It returns n if no violation. We need first index >= l. The standard code:
```
def max_right(l, f):
    if l == n: return n
    l += size
    sm = e
    while True:
        while l % 2 == 0:
            l >>= 1
        if not f(op(sm, d[l])):
            while l < size:
                l <<= 1
                if f(op(sm, d[l])):
                    sm = op(sm, d[l])
                    l += 1
            return l - size
        sm = op(sm, d[l])
        l += 1
        if (l & -l) == l:
            break
    return n
```
Here f(node_max) is true if the segment satisfies predicate. For ge, f(v) = v < x. So not f if v >= x. In code above, if max(sm, tree[l]) >= x -> fail, descend. Good. For gt, f(v)=v <= x, fail if >x.

Now, DP for a given Q:
```
def exists_dp(Q):
    dp0 = 0  # last cut position for parity 0, initially before any cut at -1? Use -1 so first r > -1 means index 0.
    dp1 = INF
    for D in Ds:
        ndp0 = ndp1 = INF
        # state c=0
        if dp0 < INF:
            r_gt = first_gt(dp0, D)  # returns index or G if none
            r_pf = first_perfect(dp0, D, target_key = 0 ^ Q)  # c^Q
            r = min(r_gt, r_pf)
            if r < G:
                nc = D & 1
                if nc == 0: ndp0 = min(ndp0, r)
                else: ndp1 = min(ndp1, r)
        # state c=1
        if dp1 < INF:
            r_gt = first_gt(dp1, D)
            r_pf = first_perfect(dp1, D, target_key = 1 ^ Q)
            r = min(r_gt, r_pf)
            if r < G:
                nc = 1 ^ (D & 1)
                if nc == 0: ndp0 = min(ndp0, r)
                else: ndp1 = min(ndp1, r)
        dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1
        if dp0 == INF and dp1 == INF: return False
    return True
